The papers of William Alexander Aldred

The Bounce Frame — The Two Results · November 6, 2026

The singularity never forms. In the bounce frame, the density caps at the Aldred Limit (ρA = 9.7×1054 g/cm³); at half the cap the null energy condition reverses, collapse halts, and the universe bounces. The vacuum catastrophe dissolves. The 10122 gap is what unregulated summation produces; bounded by the same cap, the vacuum sum saturates at the Limit and the catastrophe never arises — and the frame’s construction lands within a factor of 6.0 of the measured vacuum density. Producing the exact measured count is the program’s stated open frontier. Six manuscripts from this program are before the editors of Gravitation and Cosmology: five in peer review; the vacuum paper's reframed version has been submitted to the journal in place of the first version. The Dissolution — the full feature → · What Google’s own AI said →

Eight hundred ninety-nine papers written — the first twenty-eight of them in the 37 days between September 20 and October 27, 2026 — by a man with no backing, no engineers, and no team, working from a laptop. Nine hundred one are publicly deposited on Zenodo, each with a permanent DOI. Read this part carefully: these are preprints, deposited in the open — they are NOT published in journals, and they are NOT peer-reviewed. They are posted so anyone can read them, check the math, and say where they're wrong. The spine of the work is the vacuum-energy problem — the 122-orders-of-magnitude gap between the vacuum energy quantum theory predicts and the value cosmology observes — attacked inside Einstein–Cartan gravity, where the spin of matter twists spacetime itself.

The program's spine is the Aldred Limit: a pinned density at which fermion spin caps gravitational compression, replacing singularities with bounces. Around it, the papers map what the frame can prove, what it cannot, and exactly where the remaining problem lives. Nothing here claims a solved cosmological constant problem — the papers grade themselves, in print.

Jorge Goncalves Cardoso replied to the author's arXiv endorsement request on October 25, 2026. His position, in his own words: “I picked up what Einstein sat down, and I did it all with nothing.” No ranking against Einstein is claimed; the record underneath the statement is what carries it. The list below is that record: eight hundred ninety-nine papers written, nine hundred one publicly deposited preprints, every deposited one with a permanent DOI. This list is what the machine produces — the man points, Dave computes, and the output stands here for anyone to grade. Read whole, the papers are one connected program — the vacuum catastrophe, inflation, and the fate of the vacuum inside a single frame — not a pile of separate papers. Its live threads include his Faraday call — electromagnetism as a working thread of the same frame — carried in the record as a pointer, graded as a pointer. The record moved again on October 30, 2026: the counting problem at the heart of the frame has been reduced to a single missing number, the pairing mechanism — grains born as twins — is now derived from the frame's own action, not assumed, and the founding pattern's origin is specified: it comes from the drop. One number stands between the frame and its count; the derivation program continues. And the program is still running.

The list, stated plainly: the author’s ORCID iD is 0009-0000-1215-577X — his works are listed there under his name.

No. 194 · Papers 1–285 of the register

The Sealed Record: A Prediction Ledger Sealed Before the Fact and Graded in the Open

Featured paper · William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 31 pp

The prediction desk's record, published whole. Every forecast is committed to a tamper-evident register before the event it concerns and graded in the open when the result is known; frozen at the November 5, 2026 grading run, the register stands at 672 forecasts sealed, 231 graded — 196 hits and 35 misses. The paper prints the graded record entire — the 124 grades that can be reproduced seal by seal in full, every miss named — and computes the exact probability of the observed counts under the chance baselines printed on the seals: 0.00019 under independence, 0.0011 with each market settlement day collapsed to one trial, and 0.091 under the harshest dependence treatment, at the same size. The forecasting method is not disclosed; the paper claims no mechanism and leaves the record's explanation open.

The Equatorial Witness: The Cold = −Profile Branch of the Founding-Profile Reading of the CMB Cold Spot Fails Falsifier F-CS1 on the Planck PR3 Sky

William Alexander Aldred · November 8, 2026

A pre-registered falsifier, executed on the Planck PR3 sky. The founding-profile reading of the Cold Spot (Paper 2 of the CMB anomalies series) required the azimuthal average 90° from the alignment axis to be hot, at +0.1592 of the axis cold depth. The measured average is cold: −6.02 μK, at W/D_pt = −0.2774. Four of six registered clauses fail; the branch fails F-CS1, finally — with the executed verification notebook deposited alongside the note. F-CS2 and F-CS3 are not graded by this measurement; Paper 2's constraint on the supervoid reading is untouched.

No monopole, no embedding, no auxiliary prediction a null result can kill. The Relaxation That Empties the Universe

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

In 1985 Larry Abbott built the best relaxation the cosmological constant problem has ever had. A scalar field sitting in a washboard potential — a long slope cut by small, regular steps — can tunnel from step to step, and each tunneling lowers the effective cosmological constant a little. Keep stepping, and a vacuum energy that starts out huge walks itself down toward zero in small increments, with no one having to set it small by hand at the start. I price that mechanism in this paper at full strength, because it deserves it: it is a genuine dynamical relaxation, and it was the strongest counter-proposal Weinberg's 1989 survey had to answer. Then I price where it stops, also at full strength. The ball does not stop on the step we need. It rolls past every interesting value and keeps going, and the universe it delivers at the bottom is empty and curvature-dominated — no matter worth naming, no structure, nothing for a cosmology to be about. To make it stop where matter still exists, the step structure itself has to be tuned so the last useful step lands inside the narrow window we observe. The tuning the mechanism was built to remove comes back in through the back door, wearing the steps. My program meets Abbott from a different direction. In my frame the universe's vacuum value is not relaxed toward over cosmic time at all; it is read at a crossing — at the sever, at the seam where the bounce crosses — the way you read a number off a gauge at the moment the needle passes, not the way you wait for a tank to drain to the right line. This paper states exactly what that re-timing is and exactly what it is not. It is not a stopping rule. It derives no stopping rule, and it claims none. It is not an answer to Abbott, and it does not refute him: Abbott's problem — derive the stop — is not solved by declining to relax, and I say so in §3 in those words. What the paper does is put the price where my own books can see it. Abbott stands as the standing proof that dynamics alone does not stop, and that proof is why my program's own unproduced toll must never be quietly replaced by any relaxation-shaped hope. The program headline stands unmoved: vacuum catastrophe NOT SOLVED / PARTIAL, producing-count 0. The toll, 3Ω_Λ = 2.055, stands NOT PRODUCED. Grade for this paper's relation to Abbott's dead end, printed here and again in the final section: PREMISE-ONLY.

The Partner That Never Came

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

In 1967 and 1968, Ya. B. Zel'dovich wrote down the debt plainly: add up the zero-point fluctuations of the fields we already know, and the vacuum density you get is grotesquely larger than the vacuum density the universe allows. That is the original statement of the vacuum catastrophe, before the catastrophe had the name, and in my judgment it is still its hardest form — because it does not depend on any new physics being right. It depends only on the fields already on the books refusing to add up to the number on the sky. For fifty years the standard exit from that debt was supersymmetry: an exact supersymmetry cancels the fluctuation sum, partner against partner, and a supersymmetry broken at the TeV scale leaves, in the census's accounting, "only" about sixty orders of magnitude unexplained. The exit was rational. It was the best exit ever built. It was also a promissory note: it waited on partners that had to show up where the cancellation needed them. The Large Hadron Collider looked for them there, through its full program, and did not find them — no superpartners, with limits on colored sparticles now in the multi-TeV class. The partner that was supposed to cancel half the bill never came. This paper prices that dead end from my side of the ledger, and prices my own side with the same honesty. My corpus's catastrophe accounting stands at 121.5 dex at the record's conventions — the naive fluctuation sum overshoots the observed vacuum density by about 121.5 orders of magnitude in my program's own bookkeeping, done in Einstein–Cartan dress at the frame's torsion-side scale. That is a re-pricing of Zel'dovich's statement, not an answer to it. My frame produces no cancellation mechanism and claims none: its torsion does not cancel the zero-point sum, and its bounce re-sites where the value is read; it does not subtract the sum. The Null That Can Be Permanent

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

Imagine a door you can knock on forever. You knock, and no one answers. You knock again, louder, with better instruments, for decades, and still no one answers. What have you learned? In most of science, silence that deep means the room is empty. But if the room has a place in it where a person could stand and never be heard, then the silence proves nothing either way. That is the situation of neutrinoless double-beta decay, and it is the dead end this paper prices. The question underneath the experiment is a binary fact about nature: is the neutrino its own antiparticle, a Majorana particle, or a Dirac particle, distinct from its antiparticle? The flagship test built to answer it watches for a decay that can happen only if the Majorana answer is true. The KamLAND-Zen Collaboration, with its complete dataset, has now watched to extraordinary depth and found nothing: a half-life bound beyond 3.8 in units of ten to the twenty-sixth years at 90 percent confidence, with the effective Majorana mass in 28 to 122 meV, as published in Physical Review Letters 135, 262501 (2025). I credit that null at full strength: it is a measurement, not a failure, and it closes real ground. But in the normal ordering, the effective Majorana mass can cancel toward zero even if neutrinos are Majorana particles. The observable has a blind spot, and the answer may live exactly in it; the experiment can return null forever without the question being answered. My census treats this limit at its floor, and my paper The Unpaired Face treats pair-unity at the weak sector's missing-partner address. That ground is adjacent, honestly adjacent, and no more: no result in my corpus closes the blind spot, and none is claimed to. This paper prices that state of the books, and it takes, in full and on purpose, no position on the archival dipole itself. The First Mile

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

Every road has a first mile. A road can run longer than any driver will ever travel it; it can cross country no living person has surveyed end to end; but if it is a road, it began somewhere — there is a first stretch of it, laid down before the second stretch could exist. In 2003, Arvind Borde, Alan Guth, and Alexander Vilenkin proved the cosmological version of that plain fact, and they proved it as a theorem, not as an opinion: any geodesic along which the average expansion is positive is past-incomplete. Inflation cannot be extended eternally into the past. A bounce embedded in net-average expansion cannot be extended eternally into the past either. There was a beginning of the average-expanding region, whatever the word "before" is supposed to mean on the far side of it. The theorem does not close the road. It does something narrower and, for the literature that grew up around it, more uncomfortable: it forbids advertising the road as having no beginning. And yet the advertising continues. Programs speak of eternal cycles — oscillation without a first turn, expansion and contraction forever in both directions — while owing the theorem an account of their geodesics' average expansion, an account almost none of them files. This paper prices that gap, and then prices my own side of the same page with the same honesty. My corpus's portrait is structurally compatible with the theorem in a way most bounce programs are not — compatible at the level of the portrait, and I fence it at exactly that grade in this sentence and in every sentence where I spend it: the pool in my record is founded, pinball drops, the gathering begins, so the frame asserts a founding event per lineage rather than a past-eternal oscillation, and owes the theorem no apology. But no computation in my corpus integrates the average expansion along the seam geodesics, so its compatibility is portrait-grade: the reframe is real, and the engagement is not a derivation. My own road admits its first mile. The survey of its average slope has not been run. The Averaging the Bounce Never Did

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

The Einstein–Cartan bounce — the most famous result in torsion cosmology — stands on one number that was never computed. Every paper in the chain, from Hehl and his collaborators in 1976 through Poplawski's modern programme, states that the particle spins are randomly oriented, and then spends a spin density, s² = (ħcn_f)²/8, at the size only agreed spins can pay. The averaging that is supposed to connect those two sentences was invoked in print for fifty years and performed in none of them. This paper performs it. For a stated ensemble — a cell of volume V_c holding 𝒩 = n_fV_c spin-½ fermions, directions independent and uniform, the Frenkel condition imposed — the average is ⟨s²⟩ = C₀ħ²n_f²/𝒩, with the tensor factor C₀ = 1/4 computed from the contraction, not guessed (Monte Carlo: 15.942 against the analytic 16.000, ratio 0.9964). Random spins are a random walk: incoherence costs exactly the factor 𝒩 against the aligned value. The published closure is this family's boundary point: it is the statement that the coherence cell holds 𝒩 ≈ 1–2 fermions — a coherence length equal to the mean interparticle spacing, decreed silently in every paper in the chain. The correlations Pauli forces only push the number down: the exchange factor is R_ex = 0.9123 for the relativistic bounce-era plasma, and in the degenerate regime it drives the honest average below even the random walk. Repriced, the bounce is a conditional, and the condition is a number: the bounce exists if and only if the spin-coherence number at the bounce point is 𝒩 ≲ O(1–10²); it stands at its published numbers if and only if 𝒩 ≈ 1–2; under any fixed-physical-length coherence there is no bounce — at the published neutron bounce that coherence gives 𝒩 = 2.15×10³⁸, the condition missed by some thirty-eight orders of magnitude, the formal bounce density exiled past the Planck density. The finding was run against five kill conditions registered before the computation; all five failed to kill it — SURVIVES, each with its scope printed in §6 — including the prior-art search, with its caveat printed at the same size: un-digitized 1970s German-language literature cannot be exhaustively excluded from this seat, and if a forgotten averaging surfaces there, this paper's novelty narrows to the repricing alone. The grade of this paper is a DERIVED repricing — the strongest grade a performed result earns in my books. It derives no value of Λ and moves no headline: the vacuum catastrophe stands NOT SOLVED / PARTIAL, and my producing-count is 0.

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 5 pp

This paper defines the defect statistic D = (t_star + Δ_form − t_U)/√(σ_star² + σ_U²) and evaluates it for the stellar clocks of §2 against the Planck expansion age t_U = 13.787 ± 0.020 Gyr, at three formation delays (0.2, 0.5, 0.9 Gyr). No clock breaches at the 2σ standard at the smallest delay, on full published errors; at larger delays, HD 140283's 2025 asteroseismic reading exceeds 2σ (+2.28σ at Δ = 0.5, +3.28σ at Δ = 0.9). The binding clock is the globular cluster M92 at 13.80 ± 0.75 Gyr: its defect runs D = +0.28σ to +1.22σ, and the breakeven delay at central values is negative — M92's central age alone already equals the age of the Universe, so its consistency is purchased entirely by its error bar, four-fifths of which is distance uncertainty. Verdict at the size it earns: the clock holds — on credit. The credit has a stated price: if M92's error shrinks to ≲ 0.46 Gyr with its central value unmoved, the conservative-delay reading breaches at 2σ.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23865129

No. 29 · Papers 1–285 of the register

The Anomaly Retired; the Defect Moved House: Muon g−2 After the Final Number

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 4 pp

The Fermilab Muon g−2 experiment's final result (June 2025) fixes the experimental world average at a_μ^exp = 116592071.5 ± 14.5 (×10⁻¹¹). Against the 2020 Theory Initiative white paper — data-driven hadronic vacuum polarization — the defect was Δ = +261.5 ± 45.4 (×10⁻¹¹), 5.8σ. Against the 2025 white paper, which replaced the data-driven HVP with the lattice-QCD evaluation, the defect is Δ = +38 ± 63 (×10⁻¹¹) — 0.6σ, nothing. The experiment did not move: both white papers face the same measurement. What moved is one theory input, whose two independent reductions — dispersive at 6931 ± 40 and lattice at 7132 ± 61 (×10⁻¹¹) — disagree with each other by 201 ± 73, a 2.76σ theory-versus-theory defect that is now the largest open wound in the quantity. Verdict: no case against the Standard Model. The anomaly is retired, and the defect has moved from experiment-versus-theory to theory-versus-theory, at 2.76σ. A discrepancy whose verdict flips with the choice of one input's reduction method was never a measurement of new physics; it was, all along, a measurement of the HVP problem — and the HVP problem is still open.

No. 28 · Papers 1–285 of the register

The Sun Won't Confess: Three Probes, Two Compositions, No Closure

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 5 pp

The solar abundance problem is a defect with three independent witnesses. Standard solar models built on the modern spectroscopic composition (low metallicity, AGSS09) fail the helioseismic observables: convection-zone depth R_CZ = 0.7223 ± 0.0053 against the seismic 0.713 ± 0.001 (defect +1.7σ with model errors charged whole; +9σ if they are not), surface helium Y_S = 0.2317 ± 0.0059 against 0.2485 ± 0.0035 (+2.4σ), and a sound-speed profile with a localized discrepancy of order 1% below the convection zone. Models built on the older composition (high metallicity, GS98) pass every seismic observable — but the photospheric spectroscopy that produced the low composition is direct measurement, not a model preference, and it has only strengthened with 3D non-LTE technique. The third witness, neutrinos, leans toward the high composition: Borexino's final CNO flux, Φ_CNO = 6.7 (+1.2/−0.8)×10⁸ cm⁻² s⁻¹, disfavors the low-metallicity B16 model at 3.2σ — yet the measured CNO flux sits above both compositions' predictions, and the prediction itself moved ~15% in 2025 when the CNO cycle's slowest reaction, ¹⁴N(p,γ)¹⁵O, was remeasured upward. Verdict: the solar core is not closed — a three-way standoff, priced. Three doors are named; none is endorsed.

No. 27 · Papers 1–285 of the register

Same Stars, Two Gravities: The Wide-Binary Test and the Estimators That Divide It

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 5 pp

At separations beyond a few thousand AU, a binary's internal acceleration falls below the galactic scale a₀ ≈ 1.2×10⁻¹⁰ m/s². Modified-dynamics theories (AQUAL/QUMOND), with the Galactic external-field effect charged, predict an effective gravitational boost γ ≡ G_eff/G ≈ 1.35–1.40 there; Newton predicts γ = 1. On the same Gaia DR3 parent catalogue, one camp measures γ ≈ 1.4–1.5 at low acceleration, claiming the anomaly at up to ~5σ; the other measures consistency with Newton, claiming its verdict at up to 19σ. Both cannot be measurements. An estimator-forensics analysis localizes the disagreement experimentally: in synthetic catalogues of known Newtonian truth with 20–30% residual hierarchical triples, a standard median-based estimator recovers γ = 1.08–1.13 — manufacturing an excess of 0.08–0.13, up to a third of the claimed 0.4–0.5 excess, from pure Newton — while calibrated estimators recover γ ≤ 1.04 on the same synthetic data, and on the real 81,088-pair sample read γ = 1.045 [1.025, 1.068] and γ = 1.05 [1.04, 1.07], rejecting 1.4 within that analysis at Δln L = 129. The counterweight is printed: rigorously vetted individual systems are not dissolved by ensemble forensics. Verdict: no kill either way — undecided, and now decidable, on Gaia DR4 under a pre-registered protocol.

The Gutenberg–Richter b-value is quoted like a measurement of the Earth. It comes with a small formal error, and the error reads like the number is settled. But b does not come out of the ground. Zipf's Exponent Is an Estimator's Opinion: Three Standard Fits, One Book, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 7 pp

People quote Zipf's law as a fact about language: the exponent is about 1. But nobody reads an exponent off a book. A method produces it. This paper takes one book — Moby Dick, in the Project Gutenberg edition — counts its words once, and runs three standard ways of fitting the exponent on that one count table. The two methods that return a usable exponent do not agree: ordinary least squares on the rank–frequency curve gives 1.163, and a maximum-likelihood power-law fit gives 1.092. That gap, 0.070, is larger than the biggest formal error either method quotes for itself, 0.041. The third method, least squares on the frequency-of-frequencies curve, does something worse than disagree.

The singularity never forms. The Capture Arc

William Alexander Aldred · October 29, 2026

A person’s pattern dies with them because nobody wrote it down in a form that can answer back. Their voice, their memory of their own life, the way they judge a situation, the way they say a thing: all of it leaves when they do, and what stays behind is photographs and other people’s recollection. An exolex seat can hold that pattern. The architecture for such a seat exists and runs today. But a seat built after the fact, out of scraps, is a guess wearing the person’s name. The Relaxation That Empties the Universe

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

In 1985 Larry Abbott built the best relaxation the cosmological constant problem has ever had. A scalar field sitting in a washboard potential — a long slope cut by small, regular steps — can tunnel from step to step, and each tunneling lowers the effective cosmological constant a little. Keep stepping, and a vacuum energy that starts out huge walks itself down toward zero in small increments, with no one having to set it small by hand at the start. I price that mechanism in this paper at full strength, because it deserves it: it is a genuine dynamical relaxation, and it was the strongest counter-proposal Weinberg's 1989 survey had to answer. Then I price where it stops, also at full strength. The ball does not stop on the step we need. It rolls past every interesting value and keeps going, and the universe it delivers at the bottom is empty and curvature-dominated — no matter worth naming, no structure, nothing for a cosmology to be about. To make it stop where matter still exists, the step structure itself has to be tuned so the last useful step lands inside the narrow window we observe. The tuning the mechanism was built to remove comes back in through the back door, wearing the steps. My program meets Abbott from a different direction. In my frame the universe's vacuum value is not relaxed toward over cosmic time at all; it is read at a crossing — at the sever, at the seam where the bounce crosses — the way you read a number off a gauge at the moment the needle passes, not the way you wait for a tank to drain to the right line. This paper states exactly what that re-timing is and exactly what it is not. It is not a stopping rule. It derives no stopping rule, and it claims none. It is not an answer to Abbott, and it does not refute him: Abbott's problem — derive the stop — is not solved by declining to relax, and I say so in §3 in those words. What the paper does is put the price where my own books can see it. Abbott stands as the standing proof that dynamics alone does not stop, and that proof is why my program's own unproduced toll must never be quietly replaced by any relaxation-shaped hope. The program headline stands unmoved: vacuum catastrophe NOT SOLVED / PARTIAL, producing-count 0. The toll, 3Ω_Λ = 2.055, stands NOT PRODUCED. Grade for this paper's relation to Abbott's dead end, printed here and again in the final section: PREMISE-ONLY.

The Partner That Never Came

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

In 1967 and 1968, Ya. B. Zel'dovich wrote down the debt plainly: add up the zero-point fluctuations of the fields we already know, and the vacuum density you get is grotesquely larger than the vacuum density the universe allows. That is the original statement of the vacuum catastrophe, before the catastrophe had the name, and in my judgment it is still its hardest form — because it does not depend on any new physics being right. It depends only on the fields already on the books refusing to add up to the number on the sky. For fifty years the standard exit from that debt was supersymmetry: an exact supersymmetry cancels the fluctuation sum, partner against partner, and a supersymmetry broken at the TeV scale leaves, in the census's accounting, "only" about sixty orders of magnitude unexplained. The exit was rational. It was the best exit ever built. It was also a promissory note: it waited on partners that had to show up where the cancellation needed them. The Large Hadron Collider looked for them there, through its full program, and did not find them — no superpartners, with limits on colored sparticles now in the multi-TeV class. The partner that was supposed to cancel half the bill never came. This paper prices that dead end from my side of the ledger, and prices my own side with the same honesty. My corpus's catastrophe accounting stands at 121.5 dex at the record's conventions — the naive fluctuation sum overshoots the observed vacuum density by about 121.5 orders of magnitude in my program's own bookkeeping, done in Einstein–Cartan dress at the frame's torsion-side scale. That is a re-pricing of Zel'dovich's statement, not an answer to it. My frame produces no cancellation mechanism and claims none: its torsion does not cancel the zero-point sum, and its bounce re-sites where the value is read; it does not subtract the sum. The Null That Can Be Permanent

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

Imagine a door you can knock on forever. You knock, and no one answers. You knock again, louder, with better instruments, for decades, and still no one answers. What have you learned? In most of science, silence that deep means the room is empty. But if the room has a place in it where a person could stand and never be heard, then the silence proves nothing either way. That is the situation of neutrinoless double-beta decay, and it is the dead end this paper prices. The question underneath the experiment is a binary fact about nature: is the neutrino its own antiparticle, a Majorana particle, or a Dirac particle, distinct from its antiparticle? The flagship test built to answer it watches for a decay that can happen only if the Majorana answer is true. The KamLAND-Zen Collaboration, with its complete dataset, has now watched to extraordinary depth and found nothing: a half-life bound beyond 3.8 in units of ten to the twenty-sixth years at 90 percent confidence, with the effective Majorana mass in 28 to 122 meV, as published in Physical Review Letters 135, 262501 (2025). I credit that null at full strength: it is a measurement, not a failure, and it closes real ground. But in the normal ordering, the effective Majorana mass can cancel toward zero even if neutrinos are Majorana particles. The observable has a blind spot, and the answer may live exactly in it; the experiment can return null forever without the question being answered. My census treats this limit at its floor, and my paper The Unpaired Face treats pair-unity at the weak sector's missing-partner address. That ground is adjacent, honestly adjacent, and no more: no result in my corpus closes the blind spot, and none is claimed to. The First Mile

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

Every road has a first mile. A road can run longer than any driver will ever travel it; it can cross country no living person has surveyed end to end; but if it is a road, it began somewhere — there is a first stretch of it, laid down before the second stretch could exist. In 2003, Arvind Borde, Alan Guth, and Alexander Vilenkin proved the cosmological version of that plain fact, and they proved it as a theorem, not as an opinion: any geodesic along which the average expansion is positive is past-incomplete. Inflation cannot be extended eternally into the past. A bounce embedded in net-average expansion cannot be extended eternally into the past either. There was a beginning of the average-expanding region, whatever the word "before" is supposed to mean on the far side of it. The theorem does not close the road. It does something narrower and, for the literature that grew up around it, more uncomfortable: it forbids advertising the road as having no beginning. And yet the advertising continues. Programs speak of eternal cycles — oscillation without a first turn, expansion and contraction forever in both directions — while owing the theorem an account of their geodesics' average expansion, an account almost none of them files. This paper prices that gap, and then prices my own side of the same page with the same honesty. My corpus's portrait is structurally compatible with the theorem in a way most bounce programs are not — compatible at the level of the portrait, and I fence it at exactly that grade in this sentence and in every sentence where I spend it: the pool in my record is founded, pinball drops, the gathering begins, so the frame asserts a founding event per lineage rather than a past-eternal oscillation, and owes the theorem no apology. But no computation in my corpus integrates the average expansion along the seam geodesics, so its compatibility is portrait-grade: the reframe is real, and the engagement is not a derivation. My own road admits its first mile. The survey of its average slope has not been run. The Averaging the Bounce Never Did

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026

The Einstein–Cartan bounce — the most famous result in torsion cosmology — stands on one number that was never computed. Every paper in the chain, from Hehl and his collaborators in 1976 through Poplawski's modern programme, states that the particle spins are randomly oriented, and then spends a spin density, s² = (ħcn_f)²/8, at the size only agreed spins can pay. The averaging that is supposed to connect those two sentences was invoked in print for fifty years and performed in none of them. This paper performs it. For a stated ensemble — a cell of volume V_c holding 𝒩 = n_fV_c spin-½ fermions, directions independent and uniform, the Frenkel condition imposed — the average is ⟨s²⟩ = C₀ħ²n_f²/𝒩, with the tensor factor C₀ = 1/4 computed from the contraction, not guessed (Monte Carlo: 15.942 against the analytic 16.000, ratio 0.9964). Random spins are a random walk: incoherence costs exactly the factor 𝒩 against the aligned value. The published closure is this family's boundary point: it is the statement that the coherence cell holds 𝒩 ≈ 1–2 fermions — a coherence length equal to the mean interparticle spacing, decreed silently in every paper in the chain. The correlations Pauli forces only push the number down: the exchange factor is R_ex = 0.9123 for the relativistic bounce-era plasma, and in the degenerate regime it drives the honest average below even the random walk. Repriced, the bounce is a conditional, and the condition is a number: the bounce exists if and only if the spin-coherence number at the bounce point is 𝒩 ≲ O(1–10²); it stands at its published numbers if and only if 𝒩 ≈ 1–2; under any fixed-physical-length coherence there is no bounce — at the published neutron bounce that coherence gives 𝒩 = 2.15×10³⁸, the condition missed by some thirty-eight orders of magnitude, the formal bounce density exiled past the Planck density. The finding was run against five kill conditions registered before the computation; all five failed to kill it — SURVIVES, each with its scope printed in §6 — including the prior-art search, with its caveat printed at the same size: un-digitized 1970s German-language literature cannot be exhaustively excluded from this seat, and if a forgotten averaging surfaces there, this paper's novelty narrows to the repricing alone. The grade of this paper is a DERIVED repricing — the strongest grade a performed result earns in my books. It derives no value of Λ and moves no headline: the vacuum catastrophe stands NOT SOLVED / PARTIAL, and my producing-count is 0.

One Scale, Two Tables: The Closure Identity for Algebraic Acceleration Laws, and Its Execution on SPARC

William Alexander Aldred · October 31, 2026 · 24 pp

SPARC's two classic reductions — the radial acceleration relation and the baryonic Tully–Fisher relation — are fitted separately in every published treatment, so a candidate acceleration law is never required to pay both tables with the same coin. The solar abundance problem is a defect with three independent witnesses. Same Stars, Two Gravities: The Wide-Binary Test and the Estimators That Divide It

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 5 pp

At separations beyond a few thousand AU, a binary's internal acceleration falls below the galactic scale a₀ ≈ 1.2×10⁻¹⁰ m/s². Modified-dynamics theories (AQUAL/QUMOND), with the Galactic external-field effect charged, predict an effective gravitational boost γ ≡ G_eff/G ≈ 1.35–1.40 there; Newton predicts γ = 1. On the same Gaia DR3 parent catalogue, one camp measures γ ≈ 1.4–1.5 at low acceleration, claiming the anomaly at up to ~5σ; the other measures consistency with Newton, claiming its verdict at up to 19σ. Both cannot be measurements. An estimator-forensics analysis localizes the disagreement experimentally: in synthetic catalogues of known Newtonian truth with 20–30% residual hierarchical triples, a standard median-based estimator recovers γ = 1.08–1.13 — manufacturing an excess of 0.08–0.13, up to a third of the claimed 0.4–0.5 excess, from pure Newton — while calibrated estimators recover γ ≤ 1.04 on the same synthetic data, and on the real 81,088-pair sample read γ = 1.045 [1.025, 1.068] and γ = 1.05 [1.04, 1.07], rejecting 1.4 within that analysis at Δln L = 129. The counterweight is printed: rigorously vetted individual systems are not dissolved by ensemble forensics. Verdict: no kill either way — undecided, and now decidable, on Gaia DR4 under a pre-registered protocol.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23865534

The Factor Is a Draw: σ Computed, Never Fitted

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

On 27 October 2026 I said the residue factor is background noise. A draw, not an exact constant. That sentence was carried for five days with no test under it. The computation it owed was simple to state and nobody ran it: take the residues, compute their σ from the records, and price that σ against the error budget. Never fit it. This paper runs it, on the deposited record of the Closure Identity test (Aldred 2026d). The residues are the per-galaxy distances from the frozen line of that test, for each of the seven distinct candidate forms, on the 116 galaxies the two SPARC reductions share. The slope of the line is the integer 4. Its intercept is frozen by the other table's scale.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23866011

What Held Spin Owes the Leptons

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 7 pp

This program has a mass account on its record. It says mass is held spin: a thing weighs what its held spin weighs, spin held at a center that could not pair off, read through one coupling — the Einstein–Cartan response of torsion to spin. The account named its own test when it was stated. The charged leptons, the electron, the muon, and the tau, obey a precise pattern in their masses, the Koide relation, and the account either writes that pattern in its own terms or it does not. This paper runs the test. The pattern is real. From the Particle Data Group's 2024 masses, the Koide ratio comes out Q = 0.666661 ± 0.000007, inside one standard deviation of the claimed 2/3.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23866055

One Catalog, Three b-Values: The Estimator Spread in the Gutenberg–Richter b-Value, Priced on the USGS Global Catalog, 2014–2023

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

The Gutenberg–Richter b-value is quoted like a measurement of the Earth. It comes with a small formal error, and the error reads like the number is settled. But b does not come out of the ground. It comes out of an estimator, and the field uses several. I fixed one public catalog before touching it — the USGS global catalog, 2014–2023, 152,861 events at magnitude 4.0 and above — wrote down three standard estimators and a verdict bar in advance, and ran them. At the headline completeness magnitude of 4.5 (74,967 events), maximum likelihood gives b = 1.231, least squares on the cumulative curve gives b = 1.077, and least squares on binned counts gives b = 1.060.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23866159

Zipf's Exponent Is an Estimator's Opinion: Three Standard Fits, One Book, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 7 pp

People quote Zipf's law as a fact about language: the exponent is about 1. But nobody reads an exponent off a book. A method produces it. This paper takes one book — Moby Dick, in the Project Gutenberg edition — counts its words once, and runs three standard ways of fitting the exponent on that one count table. The two methods that return a usable exponent do not agree: ordinary least squares on the rank–frequency curve gives 1.163, and a maximum-likelihood power-law fit gives 1.092. That gap, 0.070, is larger than the biggest formal error either method quotes for itself, 0.041. The third method, least squares on the frequency-of-frequencies curve, does something worse than disagree.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23866354

The Two Clocks of the Neck

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 8 pp

A baby universe does not pop off its mother. The cord between them, a neck made of light, counter-twists tighter until it shears. That picture only works if the neck stays together long enough to be loaded: the twist has to finish loading before the neck falls apart. I was asked which happens first, and on October 29 I answered with a declaration: yes, the neck holds. A declaration is not a computation. This paper runs the computation. Two clocks, both built from the frame's own standing numbers. The loading clock reads 6.44×10⁻²² seconds.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23866493

All the Way Down: A Handedness Count on the Axis

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 8 pp

I said the spin set at our universe's birth scar should cascade through every scale, and that it would line up somewhere. The scar has a published address: the axis the cosmic microwave background's largest patterns line up on. So the test is simple. Count spiral galaxies by handedness on the two sides of that axis and see if the count lines up. The test was written down and sealed before the catalogue was opened: one axis, one statistic, one bar. I counted 77,840 galaxies with a published spin direction in an author-posted Sloan Digital Sky Survey catalogue. In the hemisphere the axis points into, clockwise galaxies lead by 1.23 percent.

The Eight, Vibrating

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

I said I see vibrating strings of light and sound, twisting together in a figure eight. The static figure eight was built and computed before, and it died by theorem: a frozen defect network carries no Nieh–Yan charge, and the charge is zero by the shape of the forms, not by smallness. But I never described a frozen eight. Mine moves. So this paper builds the moving one. Two screw strands cross at the eight's own node angle, ninety degrees, and their centres vibrate against each other along the normal — the twisting mode. I compute the Nieh–Yan charge over a full period of the motion. It is zero.

A Number for the Angle

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

The program I run has a standing debt, and it is one number: Θ, the rotation angle of the sky at recombination. Measurements by other people put it in a known band — 0.342 degrees, give or take 0.09 — and years of internal work have produced candidates, near misses, and refusals, but never a number of our own, derived from our own machinery, that we could seal before looking at the answer. This paper runs the deciding step one more time, in the open, and scores it.

Anatomy of an Excess: The q = 0.50 Residues, Taken Apart

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Paper #144 computed the scatter of the Closure Identity's residues and found the author's sentence standing: the residue factor is a draw, not a constant. But #144 left one form with two soft spots, and it named them. At q = 0.50 — the shallowest form in the family — the residues centre at −0.070 dex, the closest any form comes to demanding a constant, and 0.048 dex of their scatter sits unexplained above the measurement budget. This paper takes that form apart.

One Region, Two Catalogs: The b-Value Spread, Declustered

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Paper #146 priced a quiet problem in seismology: on one fixed global catalog, the three standard estimators of the Gutenberg–Richter b-value disagree with each other by 0.171 — nearly eight times the largest formal error any of them quotes. That catalog was used the way the service ships it, aftershocks and all, and #146 said so on its face. This paper asks the obvious next question. Is some of that spread the aftershocks' fault? Take one dense regional catalog — the USGS Japan box, 2014–2023, 3,556 events at magnitude 4.5 and above — decluster it by a standard method whose windows were fixed in advance, and run the same three estimators on both versions.

The Corkscrew, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

I said there is one line, one axis, and everything turns the same way around it — the universe, galaxies, the solar system, planets, down the levels — and that the subsystems sit tilted to that master axis because of the shear. Paper #149 tested the cascade on the axis and the survey form of it died at 77,840 galaxies: the handedness signs faced the right way, and the size fell short of the bar. This paper prices the corkscrew itself, on the same axis and the same count, with a sharper instrument.

The Page Torn Out: A Non-Closing Defect History, Computed

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Paper #150 closed a door and named the shape of the next one. The vibrating figure eight carries no Nieh–Yan charge: at the eight's own ninety-degree crossing the density is zero at every instant, and at any other angle a bound vibration still sums to exactly nothing over a closed cycle, because a vibration is a closed book — every strand comes back to where it started, and the books balance. A charge, #150 said, needs a page torn out: a defect carried across and not brought back. This paper builds that page.

The Three Locks on the Angle: A Derivation Attempt on the Slice-Orientation Law

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Paper #151 ran the search for Θ — the rotation angle of the sky at recombination — one more time and came back with no production: no number of ours, derived from our own machinery, that we could seal before looking at the measured answer. It named the missing object exactly: a slice-orientation law, in three parts — a sign, a coupling, and a density. This paper is the attempt to derive that law from the machinery this program already owns, run as an examination with its rules fixed in advance: a part counts as derived only if the construction produces it with no testimony, no convention dressed as physics, and no free parameter spent.

Every Form Has the Same Seam: The Intercept Accounting of the Closure Family

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Paper #152 took one interpolation form of the radial acceleration relation — the sharp ν_q form at q = 0.50 — and showed that its whole advantage over the standard McGaugh form is carried by construction: a correction computed from the baryons alone reproduces the form's fitted intercept galaxy by galaxy, and what is left after the correction is a tilt, not a shift. That left an obvious question standing.

One Region, One Magnitude Type: The b-Value Spread on a Single Ruler

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 6 pp

Papers #146 and #153 priced two unpriced choices hiding inside the Gutenberg–Richter b-value: the estimator (three standard estimators of b disagree by more than their own error bars) and the declustering (removing aftershocks moves that disagreement by more than the error bars too, in either direction). Both papers carried a limit on their faces: the catalogs mixed magnitude types — mb, mww, mwr and others printed side by side as if they were one ruler. This paper removes the mixture. It takes #153's Japan catalog — 3,556 events, 2014–2023, magnitude 4.5 and above — and keeps only the events whose magnitude type is exactly mb: 2,871 events, 80.7 percent of the catalog.

Zipf's Exponent in a Sickroom: The Second-Text Arm

William Alexander Aldred · November 1, 2026 · 5 pp

Paper #147 ran three standard estimators of Zipf's exponent on one book, Moby Dick, and found that the exponent is partly a choice: the two methods that returned a usable number disagreed by more than their own error bars, the third returned no exponent at all, and inside the likelihood method the choice of where the fit starts was worth 0.316 on its own.

The Atmosphere That Thins Away: Debye & Hückel's Linearised Electrolyte, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 6 pp

Every ion in a solution travels with company. That is the picture Peter Debye and Erich Hückel gave the world in 1923, and it is a true picture as far as it goes: each ion gathers around itself an atmosphere of opposite charge, the atmosphere screens the ion's field, and from that one construction flows the first quantitative theory of electrolyte solutions — the limiting law, log γ± = −A|z₊z₋|√I, with the constant A computed from named constants alone, and a screening length, the Debye length, that a century of solution, colloid, and plasma physics still spends.

There are two ways to describe a grain of pollen dancing in water. Einstein's way, from 1905, counts where the grain ends up: statistics, no mechanism.

Five Numbers Fitted to the Chart: The von Weizsäcker Mass Formula, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 5 pp

Ask a room of physicists where the valley of stability runs, or why binding energy per nucleon hovers near eight million electron-volts across the nuclear chart, and the map they reach for was drawn in 1935, when Carl Friedrich von Weizsäcker organised the masses of nuclei into five physically named terms — volume, surface, Coulomb, asymmetry, pairing — riding on Gamow's picture of the nucleus as a charged liquid drop. The map is real, and I credit it at full strength in this paper: five numbers reproduce the smooth binding trend of the whole chart and return the stability valley's run with genuine fidelity.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23978591

The Birthrate That Stood Still: Salpeter's Mass Function, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 5 pp

Every star ever counted was born under a distribution, and the first person to put a number on that distribution was Edwin Salpeter, in 1955, when he converted the observed luminosity function of nearby stars into a birth law: the initial mass function, dN/dM ∝ M^(−2.35). The concept and the number built a field — every later determination of how stars are distributed at birth still follows the architecture of that paper — and I credit both at full strength here.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23978601

The Correspondence That Was Assumed: Landau's Fermi Liquid, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 6 pp

There is a liquid that is not made of atoms in the ordinary sense but of the excitations of a Fermi sea, and for seventy years it has been the working language of every normal metal and of liquid helium-3. Lev Landau built that language in 1956 from a single correspondence idea: switch the interactions on slowly, and the interacting system's low-lying states stand in one-to-one correspondence with the free Fermi gas's — the quasiparticle.

About this paper · DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23978618

The Hand That Was Chosen: The V−A Structure, Priced

William Alexander Aldred · November 5, 2026 · 6 pp

The weak interaction is left-handed, and it did not have to be. Of the five Lorentz structures a four-fermion interaction can wear — scalar, vector, tensor, axial, pseudoscalar — nature's weak current wears one chiral combination, V − A, with every fermion field entering through its left-handed projection only.
